2. **Factorization of Numerator:**  
   Recognize that \( x^3 - 1 \) can be factored using the difference of cubes formula:  
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**  
   After factorization, the expression becomes:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
   \]
   Provided \( x \neq 1 \), the \( (x - 1) \) terms cancel out.

   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

   \[

***********************<think>
Sure, let's solve this limit problem step by step.

**Problem:** Find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} \).

**Solution:**

We need to evaluate:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2}
\]

**Step 1:** Observe the Behavior as \( x \) Approaches 0

First, let's understand what happens when we directly substitute \( x = 0 \):

\[
\frac{1 - \cos(0)}{0^2} = \frac{1 - 1}{0} = \frac{0}{0}
\]

This results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \). Therefore, we cannot directly evaluate the limit by substitution. We need another method to resolve this.

**Step 2:** Use a Trigonometric Identity

Recall the trigonometric identity:
\[
1 - \cos(x) = 2 \sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

This identity helps us rewrite the numerator in terms of sine, which may simplify our limit.

**Step 3:** Substitute the Identity into the Limit

Substitute \( 1 - \cos(x) \) with \( 2 \sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right) \):

\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{2 \sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)}{x^2}
\]

**Step 4:** Simplify the Expression

We can factor out the constant from the numerator:

\[
2 \cdot \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)}{x^2}
\]

Notice that \( x = 2 \cdot \left(\frac{x}{2}\right) \). Let's make a substitution to simplify further. Let \( t = \frac{x}{2} \), so as \( x \to 0 \), \( t \to 0 \).

Now, the limit becomes:

\[
2 \cdot \lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin^2(t)}{(2t)^2}
\]

Simplify \( (2t)^2 = 4t^2 \):

\[
2 \cdot \lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin^2(t)}{4t^2} = 2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{4} \cdot \lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin^2(t)}{t^2} \right) 
\]

**Step 5:** Evaluate the Limit

Recall that:
\[
\lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin(t)}{t} = 1
\]

Therefore:

\[
\lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin^2(t)}{t^2} = \left( \lim_{{t \to 0}} \frac{\sin(t)}{t} \right)^2 = 1^2 = 1
\]

**Step 6:** Combine the Results

Substitute this back into our expression:

\[
2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{4} \cdot 1 \right) = \frac{2}{4} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

**Conclusion:**

The limit is \( \frac{1}{2} \).

The problem involves finding the limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side. By rewriting and applying logarithmic properties, we determine that this limit equals 1.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Problem Statement**: Evaluate \( \lim_{{x \to 0+}} x^x \).

2. **Rewrite Using Logarithms**:
   - Let \( y = x^x \).
   - Take natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).

3. **Evaluate the Limit of \( x \cdot \ln(x) \)** as \( x \to 0+ \):
   - As \( x \to 0+ \), \( \ln(x) \to -\infty \).
   - However, multiplying by \( x \to 0 \) results in an indeterminate form \( 0 \cdot (-\infty) \).

4. **Apply Transformation for L’Hospital’s Rule**:
   - Rewrite as \( \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \), which tends to \( \frac{-\infty}{\infty} \).
   - Differentiate numerator and denominator: 
     - \( \frac{d}{dx}\ln(x) = 1/x \),
     - \( \frac{d}{dx}(1/x) = -1/x^2 \).

5. **Apply L’Hospital’s Rule**:
   - \( \lim_{{x \to 0+}} \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = \lim_{{x \to 0+}} (-x) = 0 \).

6. **Exponentiate Result**:
   - Since \( \ln(y) = 0 \), then \( y = e^0 = 1 \).

Final Answer: The limit is 1.

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1^-}} \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)^x
\]

\[

\]

\[

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} \) at \( x = 1 \), we start by expressing the function in a form that allows us to apply limit rules. 

We need to compute the limit:
\[
f'(1) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + (1+h)^3} - \sqrt[3]{2}}{h}
\]

First, we simplify \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} \). The derivative of this function can be found using the chain rule. Let \( y = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \), then:
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
\]

Evaluating this derivative at \( x = 1 \):
\[
\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right|_{x=1} = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^{3})^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}}
\]

We can express \( (2)^{2/3} \) as the cube root of 4, so:
\[
\frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

Thus, the limit is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
\]

First, differentiate each term separately.

The derivative of 3x² is found by multiplying the coefficient by the exponent and then reducing the exponent by one, resulting in 6x.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   
   - For \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - For \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - For the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   
   Adding up the derivatives of each term gives:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]
   
3. **Final Answer**:
   
   The derivative of \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5
   \]

So, the final answer is:

\boxed{f'(x) = 6x - 5}

Next, for v(x) = e^x, the derivative of e raised to any function is itself times the derivative of that function. Since the exponent here is just x, whose derivative is also 1, the derivative of v(x) remains e^x. Therefore, v'(x) = e^x.

F'(x) = 1 · e^x + x · e^x

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \), we use the product rule. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Components:**
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^{x} \)

2. **Find the Derivatives of Each Component:**
   - \( u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)
   - \( v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x}) = e^{x} \)

3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   The product rule states that:
   \[
   (u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
   \]
   
   Substituting the derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the Values:**
   \[
   F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^{x} + x \cdot e^{x}
   \]

5. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Factor out \( e^{x} \):
   \[
   F'(x) = (1 + x) \cdot e^{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
F'(x) = (1 + x)e^{x}

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll proceed step by step.

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))
We'll use the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) = x^2 \) and \( v(x) = e^x \), then:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
First, compute \( u'(x) \) and \( v'(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = 2x \)
- \( v'(x) = e^x \)

Now apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = (2x)e^x + x^2 e^x
\]
Simplify by factoring out \( e^x \):
\[
F'(x) = e^x (2x + x^2)
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))
Now, differentiate \( F'(x) \) again using the product rule. Let:
- \( u_1(x) = x^2 + 2x \)
- \( v_1(x) = e^x \)

First, compute \( u_1'(x) \) and \( v_1'(x) \):
- \( u_1'(x) = 2x + 2 \)
- \( v_1'(x) = e^x \)

Apply the product rule:
\[
F''(x) = (2x + 2)e^x + (x^2 + 2x)e^x
\]
Simplify by factoring out \( e^x \):
\[
F''(x) = e^x (2x + 2 + x^2 + 2x)
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:

1. Derivative of the outer function: \( -1 \times (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \)
2. Multiply by the derivative of the inner function: \( 2x \)

Combining these gives:
\[ F'(x) = -2x / (x^2 + 1)^2 \]

The derivative of \(\ln(x^2)\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using the chain rule. 

1. Let \(u = x^2\).
2. Then, \(\frac{d}{dx}[\ln(u)] = \frac{1}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}\).
3. Compute \(\frac{du}{dx} = 2x\).
4. Substitute back: \(\frac{1}{x^2} \cdot 2x = \frac{2x}{x^2} = \frac{2}{x}\).

**Final Answer**
The second derivative of the function is \boxed{\pi \left(i - \pi x^2\right) e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)}}.
</think>

Given the function \( f(x) = e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \), we need to find its second derivative.

First, compute the first derivative using the chain rule:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{g(x)} \right) \]
where \( g(x) = i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1) \). The derivative of \( g(x) \) is:
\[ g'(x) = i \frac{\pi}{2} \cdot 2x = i \pi x \]

Thus, the first derivative is:
\[ f'(x) = g'(x) e^{g(x)} = i \pi x e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \]

Next, compute the second derivative by differentiating \( f'(x) \):
\[ f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( i \pi x e^{g(x)} \right) \]
Using the product rule:
\[ f''(x) = i \pi e^{g(x)} + i \pi x \cdot g'(x) e^{g(x)} \]

Substitute \( g'(x) = i \pi x \):
\[ f''(x) = i \pi e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} + i \pi x \cdot (i \pi x) e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \]

Simplify the terms:
\[ f''(x) = i \pi e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} + (i^2)(\pi^2 x^2) e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \]
Since \( i^2 = -1 \):
\[ f''(x) = i \pi e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} - \pi^2 x^2 e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \]

Factor out \( \pi \):
\[ f''(x) = \pi \left( i - \pi x^2 \right) e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)} \]

Thus, the second derivative of the function is:
\[
\boxed{\pi \left(i - \pi x^2\right) e^{i \frac{\pi}{2} (x^2 + 1)}}
\]

Wait, what's the quotient rule again? It's something like the denominator times the derivative of the numerator minus the numerator times the derivative of the denominator, all over the denominator squared. So if F(x) is u/v, then F'(x) = (v*u' - u*v') / v². Got it.

The derivative of the inverse secant function with respect to \( x \) is given by:

\frac{d}{dx} (\operatorname{arcsec}(x)) = \frac{1}{|x| \sqrt{x^2 - 1}}

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \) is a well-known result in calculus. Here are three methods that can be used to evaluate it:

### Method 1: Using L’Hospital’s Rule
Since substituting \( x = 0 \) results in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \), we can apply L’Hospital’s Rule, which states that if \( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \) is an indeterminate form of type \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
\[ \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} \]
provided the limit on the right exists.

For \( f(x) = \sin(x) \) and \( g(x) = x \):
- The derivative of \( f(x) \) is \( f'(x) = \cos(x) \).
- The derivative of \( g(x) \) is \( g'(x) = 1 \).

Applying L’Hospital’s Rule:
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos(x)}{1} = \cos(0) = 1 \]

### Method 2: Using the Taylor Series Expansion
The Taylor series expansion of \( \sin(x) \) around \( x = 0 \) is:
\[ \sin(x) = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \frac{x^5}{120} - \cdots \]

Dividing both sides by \( x \):
\[ \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 - \frac{x^2}{6} + \frac{x^4}{120} - \cdots \]

Taking the limit as \( x \to 0 \), all terms containing powers of \( x \) approach zero, leaving:
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 \]

### Method 3: Using the Squeeze Theorem
Consider the inequality for small positive angles (in radians):
\[ \cos(x) \leq \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \leq 1 \]
As \( x \to 0 \), both \( \cos(x) \) and \( 1 \) approach \( 1 \). By the Squeeze Theorem, the middle expression must also approach \( 1 \):
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 \]

### Conclusion
Using any of these methods, we find that:
\[ \boxed{1} \]

The problem requires evaluating the limit as x approaches 0 of (1 - cos(x)) divided by x squared.

**Solution:**
We can use the Taylor series expansion of cos(x) around 0:
- cos(x) = 1 - (x²)/2 + higher-order terms.

Substituting this into the expression, we get:
(1 - [1 - (x²)/2]) / x² = (x²/2) / x² = 1/2.

Thus, the limit is 1/2.

The problem involves finding the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the expression \( \frac{3x - \ln(x)}{5x + \ln(x)} \). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Behavior of Each Term:**  
   As \( x \) becomes very large, logarithmic terms like \( \ln(x) \) grow much slower compared to linear terms (e.g., \( 3x \) and \( 5x \)). This means that as \( x \to \infty \), the \( \ln(x) \) terms become negligible in comparison.

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by \( x \):**  
   To simplify, divide both numerator and denominator by \( x \):
   \[
   \frac{3x - \ln(x)}{5x + \ln(x)} = \frac{\frac{3x}{x} - \frac{\ln(x)}{x}}{\frac{5x}{x} + \frac{\ln(x)}{x}} = \frac{3 - \frac{\ln(x)}{x}}{5 + \frac{\ln(x)}{x}}
   \]
   
3. **Evaluate the Limit of Each Part:**  
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{\ln(x)}{x} \) approaches 0 because logarithmic growth is slower than linear growth.
   Therefore:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(3 - \frac{\ln(x)}{x}\right) = 3 \\
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(5 + \frac{\ln(x)}{x}\right) = 5
   \]
   
4. **Compute the Final Limit:**  
   Plugging these results back into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3 - 0}{5 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

**Conclusion:**
The limit is \( \frac{3}{5} \).

One common approach for evaluating limits involving trigonometric functions is using L'Hospital's Rule. This rule applies when we have an indeterminate form of type \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \). 

So, let's apply L'Hospital's Rule to our problem:

1. **Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):**
   - The derivative of \( \sin x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \cos x \).
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{1}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the limit after differentiation:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, \( \cos x \) approaches \( \cos(0) = 1 \).
   - Therefore, the limit becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
     \]

\[

we can use **L'Hospital's Rule**, which applies when evaluating limits that result in an indeterminate form such as \( \frac{0}{0} \).

1. **Check the Indeterminate Form:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, both the numerator (\( \sin x \)) and the denominator (\( x \)) approach 0.
   - Therefore, the limit is in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
     - The derivative of \( \sin x \) is \( \cos x \).
     - The derivative of \( x \) is 1.

     \[

   - Therefore:

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \) can be evaluated using L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach 0. Applying the rule, we take the derivatives of the numerator and denominator:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1 + x)}{\frac{d}{dx}x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

**Answer:**

Okay, so I need to find the limit as x approaches 0 of (x - tan(x))/x³. Hmm, let's see how to approach this.

First off, when dealing with limits that result in an indeterminate form like 0/0 or ∞/∞, L'Hospital's Rule is a good tool to use. But before jumping into that, maybe I should try expanding tan(x) using its Taylor series around 0 to see if that simplifies things.

I remember that the Taylor series expansion for tan(x) around 0 starts with x + (x³)/3 + higher-order terms. So, let me write that down:

tan(x) = x + (x³)/3 + (2x⁵)/15 + ... 

So if I subtract tan(x) from x, it becomes:

x - tan(x) = x - [x + (x³)/3 + (2x⁵)/15 + ...] 
= x - x - (x³)/3 - (2x⁵)/15 - ...
= - (x³)/3 - (2x⁵)/15 - ...

Now, the expression we're taking the limit of is:

(x - tan(x))/x³ = [ - (x³)/3 - (2x⁵)/15 - ... ] / x³ 
= (-1/3) - (2x²)/15 - ...

As x approaches 0, all the terms with higher powers of x will approach 0. So we're left with:

limₓ→₀ [(-1/3) - (2x²)/15 - ...] = -1/3

Alternatively, I could use L'Hospital's Rule since both numerator and denominator approach 0 as x approaches 0.

Let me try that method too to verify. The limit is in the form 0/0, so applying L'Hospital once:

limₓ→₀ [d/dx (x - tan(x))] / [d/dx (x³)] 
= limₓ→₀ [1 - sec²(x)] / [3x²]

Now, as x approaches 0, sec²(0) = 1, so the numerator becomes 1 - 1 = 0 and denominator is also 0. So we have another indeterminate form 0/0, which means applying L'Hospital's Rule a second time.

Taking derivatives again:

Numerator derivative: d/dx [1 - sec²(x)] = -2sec²(x)tan(x)
Denominator derivative: d/dx [3x²] = 6x

So the limit now becomes:

limₓ→₀ [ -2sec²(x)tan(x) ] / [6x ]

Simplify constants and factor out the negative sign:

= (-2/6) limₓ→₀ [sec²(x) tan(x)] / x
= (-1/3) limₓ→₀ [sec²(x) (sin(x)/cos(x))] / x

Since sec(x) = 1/cos(x), this becomes:

= (-1/3) limₓ→₀ [ (1/cos²(x)) * sin(x)/cos(x) ) ] / x
= (-1/3) limₓ→₀ [ sin(x) / (x cos³(x)) ]

We know that as x approaches 0, sin(x) ≈ x and cos(x) ≈ 1. So substituting these approximations:

≈ (-1/3) limₓ→₀ [ x / (x * 1 ) ] 
= (-1/3) limₓ→₀ [1] = -1/3

Both methods gave me the same result, which is reassuring.

I could also use Taylor series expansion as I did initially and it's straightforward. The key was to recognize that expanding tan(x) would allow simplification of the numerator, making the limit easy to compute by cancelling out x³ terms.

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln x}{x} \) can be determined using analysis:

1. **Growth Rates**: The natural logarithm function (\(\ln x\)) grows much slower than linear functions (like \(x\)). Therefore, as \( x \) becomes very large, the denominator \( x \) will dominate, causing the fraction to approach zero.

2. **L'Hospital's Rule**:
   - Original limit: \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\ln x}{x} \)
   - Both numerator (\(\ln x\)) and denominator (\(x\)) approach infinity, fitting the form for L'Hospital's Rule.
   - Apply derivatives:
     - Derivative of numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
     - Derivative of denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)
   - New limit after applying L'Hospital's Rule: \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{1}{x} = 0 \)

Both methods confirm that the limit is zero.

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of the expression:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - \sqrt[3]{2}}{x - 1}
\]

we recognize that this is a derivative evaluated at \( x = 1 \). Specifically, it represents the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} \) at \( x = 1 \).

First, we rewrite the cube root as a power:

\[
f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3}
\]

To find the derivative \( f'(x) \), we use the chain rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \right) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}
\]

Evaluating this derivative at \( x = 1 \):

\[
f'(1) = 1^2 \cdot (1 + 1^3)^{-2/3} = 1 \cdot (2)^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}}
\]

Recognizing that \( (2)^{2/3} \) is the cube root of 4, we can express this as:

\[
\frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

Thus, the limit is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we apply the power rule to each term:

1. For \( 3x^2 \):
   - Multiply the exponent (2) by the coefficient (3): \( 2 \times 3 = 6 \).
   - Reduce the exponent by 1: \( x^{2-1} = x \).
   - Result: \( 6x \).

2. For \( -5x \):
   - The exponent of \( x \) is implicitly 1.
   - Multiply by the coefficient (-5): \( 1 \times (-5) = -5 \).
   - Reduce the exponent by 1: \( x^{1-1} = x^0 = 1 \).
   - Result: \( -5 \).

3. For the constant term \( +4 \):
   - The derivative of a constant is 0.

Combining these results, the derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = 6x - 5
\]

The problem involves finding the limit as \( h \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\frac{1}{x+h} - \frac{1}{x}}{h} \), which represents the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \).

1. Compute the difference quotient:
   \[
   \frac{\frac{1}{x + h} - \frac{1}{x}}{h} = \frac{x - (x + h)}{h \cdot x(x + h)} = \frac{-h}{h \cdot x(x + h)} = \frac{-1}{x^2 + xh}
   \]
   
2. Take the limit as \( h \) approaches 0:
   \[
   \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{x^2 + xh} = \frac{-1}{x^2}
   \]

The final answer is \(-\frac{1}{x^2}\).

The problem requires finding the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \).

To solve this:
1. Recall the rule for differentiating an exponential function: The derivative of \( a^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \ln(a) \cdot a^x \).
2. For \( e^x \), where \( e \) is the base, \( \ln(e) = 1 \). Therefore, applying the rule gives \( \frac{d}{dx} e^x = e^x \).

Thus, the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we will apply basic differentiation rules to each term individually.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   - The derivative of \( x^3 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 3x^2 \).
   - The derivative of \( 2x^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 4x \).
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 1 \).
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 1 \) is \( 0 \).

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   \[
   f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1}

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
Differentiate each term separately with respect to \( x \):

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3
\]
\[
\frac{d}{dx}(-3x^3) = -9x^2
\]

So, the first derivative \( f'(x) \) is:
\[
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Differentiate \( f'(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 12x^2
\]
\[
\frac{d}{dx}(-9x^2) = -18x
\]

Therefore, the second derivative \( f''(x) \) is:
\[
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
\]

### Final Answer

The second derivative of the function is:

\[
\boxed{f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[ F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

Simplify the expression:

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x^x \):

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).
3. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot y' \).
   - Right side (using product rule): \( \ln(x) + 1 \).
4. Solve for \( y' \): \( y' = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
5. Substitute back \( y = x^x \): \( f'(x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

Differentiate F with respect to u: dF/du = 1/u.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln x^2 \), we can use logarithmic properties and the chain rule.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Simplify the Function Using Logarithmic Properties:**
   
   We can rewrite \( F(x) = \ln x^2 \) using the power rule of logarithms:
   \[
   F(x) = 2 \cdot \ln x
   \]

2. **Differentiate the Simplified Function:**
   
   Now, differentiate \( F(x) = 2 \cdot \ln x \) with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   F'(x) = 2 \cdot \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x)
   \]
   
3. **Apply the Derivative of \( \ln x \):**
   
   The derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \):
   \[
   F'(x) = 2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
   \]

4. **Simplify the Result:**
   
   Multiply to get the final derivative:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{2}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{2}{x}}

The derivative of arctan(x) with respect to x is found using differentiation techniques and results in \( \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. Let \( y = \arctan(x) \).
2. Rewrite the equation as \( \tan(y) = x \).
3. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Using the chain rule on the left side, we get \( \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \).
4. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
   - \( \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)} \).
5. Express \( \sec^2(y) \) in terms of \( x \):
   - Since \( y = \arctan(x) \), we can use the identity \( \sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y) = 1 + x^2 \).
6. Substitute back:
   - \( \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \).

To find the derivative of \( \frac{\sin(x)}{x^2 + 1} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{v(x) \cdot u'(x) - u(x) \cdot v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = \sin(x) \\
   v(x) = x^2 + 1
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = \cos(x) \\
   v'(x) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   \[
   h'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1) \cdot \cos(x) - \sin(x) \cdot (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression if needed:**
   The expression is already simplified.

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos(x) - 2x\sin(x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

The second derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) is found by first taking the first derivative using the chain rule, then applying the product rule to find the second derivative.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (f'(x))**:
   - Given \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \).
   - Let \( u = x^2 \), so \( f(x) = e^u \).
   - Using the chain rule, \( f'(x) = e^u \cdot \frac{du}{dx} \).
   - Compute \( \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \).
   - Therefore, \( f'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2} \).

2. **Second Derivative (f''(x))**:
   - We now differentiate \( f'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \).
   - Let’s use the product rule: if \( h(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then \( h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \).
   - Here, \( u(x) = 2x \) and \( v(x) = e^{x^2} \).
   - Compute \( u'(x) = 2 \) and \( v'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \) (using the chain rule again for \( v(x) \)).
   - Apply the product rule:
     \[
     f''(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot 2x e^{x^2}
     \]
   - Simplify each term:
     - First term: \( 2e^{x^2} \)
     - Second term: \( 4x^2 e^{x^2} \)
   - Combine the terms:
     \[
     f''(x) = (2 + 4x^2) e^{x^2}
     \]
   - Factor out a 2 for simplicity:
     \[
     f''(x) = 2(1 + 2x^2) e^{x^2}
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The second derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) is \( (4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2} \).

The problem involves finding the derivative of a rational function \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \). To solve this, we can use either the quotient rule or rewrite the function and apply the product rule with the chain rule. Both methods lead to the same result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function:**  
   The given function is \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \).

2. **Choose a Differentiation Method:**
   - **Option 1 (Quotient Rule):** Use \( u = x \) and \( v = 1 + x^2 \).
   - **Option 2 (Product Rule with Chain Rule):** Rewrite as \( F(x) = x \cdot (1 + x^2)^{-1} \).

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   - Compute derivatives:
     - \( u' = 1 \)
     - \( v' = 2x \)
   - Apply the formula:
     \[
     F'(x) = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 + x^2 - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
     \]

4. **Verify Using the Product Rule:**
   - Let \( u = x \) and \( v = (1 + x^2)^{-1} \).
   - Compute derivatives:
     - \( u' = 1 \)
     - \( v' = -1 \cdot (1 + x^2)^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(1 + x^2)^2} \)
   - Apply the product rule:
     \[
     F'(x) = u'v + uv' = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} + x \left(-\frac{2x}{(1 + x^2)^2}\right) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
     \]

5. **Final Answer:**
   The derivative of \( F(x) \) is:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': 'The derivative of F(x) is (1 - x²)/(1 + x²)²'}

(1/2)sin x < (1/2)x < (1/2)tan x

Multiplying all parts by 2/sin x gives:

x cot x < 1 < (cos x)/x * sin x

1 < x / sin x < tan x / sin x

Simplify tan x as sin x / cos x, so tan x / sin x is 1/cos x.

So now we have 1 < x/sin x < 1/cos x

As x approaches 0 from the right, cos x approaches 1. So taking reciprocals (remembering that reversing inequalities when multiplying by negative numbers isn't needed here since all terms are positive):

cos x < sin x / x < 1

But wait, as x approaches 0, both sides approach 1. By the squeeze theorem, the limit of sin x / x must also be 1.

a³ - b³ = (a - b)(a² + ab + b²)

Applying this to x³ - 1, where a = x and b = 1, we get:

x³ - 1 = (x - 1)(x² + x*1 + 1²) = (x - 1)(x² + x + 1)

So now, the numerator is factored into (x - 1)(x² + x + 1). The denominator is just (x - 1), so I can write the function as:

(x³ - 1)/(x - 1) = [(x - 1)(x² + x + 1)]/(x - 1)

Since (x - 1) is a common factor in both the numerator and denominator, I can cancel them out. However, it's important to note that we're taking the limit as x approaches 1, not at x = 1, so cancelling is permissible here.

lim(x→1) (x² + x + 1)

- 1² = 1
- x = 1

Therefore, the limit as x approaches 1 of (x³ - 1)/(x - 1) is 3.

The problem asks for the limit of \(\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\) as \(x\) approaches 1.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Direct Substitution:**  
   When we directly substitute \(x = 1\), both the numerator and denominator become zero, resulting in an indeterminate form \(\frac{0}{0}\).

2. **Factorization:**  
   To resolve the indeterminate form, factorize the numerator using the difference of cubes formula:  
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Expression:**  
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel the common factor \((x - 1)\):
   \[
   \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1
   \]
   
4. **Evaluate the Limit:**  
   Now, compute the limit as \(x\) approaches 1:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The value of the limit is **3**.

The limit evaluates to a finite value as \( x \) approaches 0 from both sides because the function is well-behaved near zero.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Limit:** We need to find \( \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \).

2. **Recognize Standard Limits or Series Expansion:**
   - The expression resembles a standard limit that can be evaluated using known series expansions.

3. **Use Taylor Series Expansion:**
   - Expand \( \ln(1+x) \) as \( x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \dots \).

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   - Divide each term by \( x \): \( 1 - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{x^2}{3} - \dots \).
   - As \( x \to 0 \), higher power terms vanish, leaving the limit as 1.

5. **Alternative Method Using Derivative Definition:**
   - Recognize that the limit is equivalent to evaluating \( f'(0) \) where \( f(x) = \ln(1+x) \).
   - Compute derivative \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x} \), so \( f'(0) = 1 \).

6. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule (Alternative Approach):**
   - Both numerator and denominator approach 0, so differentiate numerator and denominator.
   - Derivative of numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+x) = \frac{1}{1 + x} \).
   - Derivative of denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}x = 1 \).
   - Applying the rule gives \( \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1} = 1 \).

**Answer:** The limit is 1.

The limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side can be found by rewriting the expression using natural logarithms and applying L'Hospital's Rule to resolve the indeterminate form.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express the Function with Natural Logarithm:**
   - Start by rewriting \( x^x = e^{x \ln x} \).
   
2. **Evaluate the Limit of the Exponent:**
   - Find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \ln x
     \]
   
3. **Rewrite to Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Recognize that this is an indeterminate form \( 0 \times (-\infty) \), so rewrite it as:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln x}{1/x}
     \]
   
4. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and denominator:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}, \quad \frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{1}{x} \right) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
     \]
   - The limit becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{-\frac{1}{x^2}} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} -x = 0
     \]

5. **Combine Results:**
   - Substitute back into the original expression:
     \[
     e^{0} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**

The limit is \( 1 \).

To find the limit:

$$
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1}
$$

we can apply L’Hospital’s Rule since substituting \( x = 1 \) results in an indeterminate form \( 0/0 \).

Applying L’Hospital’s Rule involves differentiating the numerator and denominator separately.

Let:
- Numerator: \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \)
- Denominator: \( g(x) = x - 1 \)

First, find the derivatives:
- \( f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left((1 + x^3)^{1/3}\right) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}} \)
- \( g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x - 1) = 1 \)

Applying L’Hospital’s Rule:
$$
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
$$

Substituting \( x = 1 \):
$$
\frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
$$

Thus, the limit is:

$$
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
$$

The derivative of Eഫ് (�ക്സ്) = tan(എക്സ്) is calculated by finding the derivative of tan(x), which is known to be sec²x.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function:** The given function is f(x) = tan(x).

2. **Apply Derivative Rules:**
   - Use the quotient rule for differentiation since tan(x) can be expressed as sin(x)/cos(x).
   - Let g(x) = sin(x) and h(x) = cos(x). Their derivatives are g'(x) = cos(x) and h'(x) = -sin(x).

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule Formula:**
   f'(x) = [g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)] / [h(x)]²
   Substituting the values:
   f'(x) = [cos(x)*cos(x) - sin(x)*(-sin(x))] / cos²(x)
   
4. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   The numerator becomes cos²x + sin²x, which equals 1 (using the Pythagorean identity).

5. **Final Derivative:**
   f'(x) = 1 / cos²x, which is equivalent to sec²x.

The derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \) is found using the product rule, which states that the derivative of a product of two functions is the derivative of the first times the second plus the first times the derivative of the second. 

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)  
  Then, \( u'(x) = 1 \)

- \( v(x) = e^{x} \)  
  Then, \( v'(x) = e^{x} \)

Applying the product rule:  
\( F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \)  
\( F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^{x} + x \cdot e^{x} \)  
Factor out \( e^{x} \):  
\( F'(x) = (1 + x)e^{x} \)

Final Answer:  
The derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \) is \( (1 + x)e^{x} \).

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is found by applying the product rule twice.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x)):**
   - Let \( u(x) = x^2 \) and \( v(x) = e^x \).
   - Using the product rule:
     \[
     F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
     \]
   - Calculate derivatives:
     \[
     u'(x) = 2x, \quad v'(x) = e^x
     \]
   - Substitute back:
     \[
     F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x = e^x (x^2 + 2x)
     \]

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x)):**
   - Let \( f(x) = e^x \) and \( g(x) = x^2 + 2x \).
   - Again, apply the product rule:
     \[
     F''(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)
     \]
   - Compute derivatives:
     \[
     f'(x) = e^x, \quad g'(x) = 2x + 2
     \]
   - Substitute into the formula:
     \[
     F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 2x) + e^x (2x + 2)
     \]
   - Factor out \( e^x \):
     \[
     F''(x) = e^x [x^2 + 2x + 2x + 2] = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:
\[
F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
\]

\[

- \( \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \)

The derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is given by:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(x)) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \):**
   - This implies that \( \tan(y) = x \).

2. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Using the chain rule on the left side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [\tan(y)] = \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - The derivative of the right side is simply 1, since \( \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1 \).

3. **Set up the equation:**
   \[
   \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]

4. **Express \( \sec^2(y) \) in terms of \( x \):**
   - Since \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \), we can consider a right triangle where the opposite side is \( x \) and the adjacent side is 1.
   - The hypotenuse of this triangle is \( \sqrt{1 + x^2} \).
   - Therefore, \( \sec(y) = \frac{\text{hypotenuse}}{\text{adjacent}} = \frac{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}{1} \), so:
     \[
     \sec^2(y) = (\sqrt{1 + x^2})^2 = 1 + x^2
     \]

5. **Substitute back into the equation:**
   \[
   (1 + x^2) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]

6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{1 + x^{2}}}
\]

\[ f'(x) = \frac{g'(x) h(x) - g(x) h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} \]

- The derivative of \( h(x) = 1 + x^2 \) is \( h'(x) = 2x \).

\[ f'(x) = \frac{1 \cdot (1 + x^2) - x \cdot 2x}{(1 + x^2)^2} \]

Simplifying the numerator:
- \( 1 \cdot (1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2 \)
- \( x \cdot 2x = 2x^2 \)

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x) h(x) - g(x) h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( g(x) \) and \( h(x) \)**
- Let \( g(x) = x \)
- Let \( h(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( g(x) \) and \( h(x) \)**
- The derivative of \( g(x) = x \) is:
\[
g'(x) = 1
\]
- The derivative of \( h(x) = 1 + x^2 \) is:
\[
h'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**
Substitute \( g(x) \), \( g'(x) \), \( h(x) \), and \( h'(x) \) into the quotient rule formula:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x) h(x) - g(x) h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{1(1 + x^2) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify the numerator:

\[
1(1 + x^2) - x(2x) = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

So, the derivative becomes:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
